* **The idea**: Keep the all-sites wave function, which has the full sign capacity by definition ($\rho\equiv1$), and its cheapest engine (R13.1), and score it at $L=32$, where the $\mathcal{O}(N_e^3)$ against $\mathcal{O}(N_e^4)$ class difference per sweep should finally show; R13.1′ left $L\ge32$ open.
* **Lineage**: Operation A on R13.1′ after its T1 kill at $L=24$, Round 15. No successor.
* **Outcome**: KILLED at G4 (2026-11-17, T0).
* **Kill rule**: Gate 4: does the measured size trend reach a scaled bar ($R_D-\sigma\ge3$ at $L=16$ and $\ge4.5$ at $L=24$ extend to $\ge6$ at $L=32$; a generous fixed bar is 4.5)?
* **Evidence**: low-noise sweep-time ratios against `dense2` of 3.54 at $L=16$ ($N=112$) and 3.89 at $L=24$ ($N=252$): $N$ grew 2.25 times while the ratio grew 1.10 times, an effective exponent of 0.12 instead of the class exponent 1. Extrapolated to $L=32$ ($N=448$): $3.89\cdot(448/252)^{0.12}\approx4.2$, before the integrated autocorrelation time, which at $L=24$ was worse for the route ($1.60\pm0.36$ against $0.83\pm0.27$ sweeps).
* **Why this kills it**: the dense LU gains efficiency with $N$ faster than the route's BLAS-2 products, so the measured gain falls short of even the fixed 4.5 bar at $L=32$, and the scaled bar is 6.
* **What the kill does not rule out**: hardware where the dense factorisation does not gain efficiency with $N$ (or where batched ratios turn the route's BLAS-2 work into matrix–matrix products); sizes far beyond $L=32$, which no accuracy test here could reach.
* **Strongest case against the kill**: a two-point exponent is a weak extrapolation, and LU efficiency saturates at some $N$; unresolved, but a direct $L=32$ timing (about 1 CPU-hour per arm) is predicted to miss by a factor of about 1.4, which fails the compute gate's G2.
* **Checked by**: self-gated by the round-15 route generator (2026-11-17); no per-leaf critic yet.
* **Lesson**: L47 (`library.md`).

### R15.4 — Domain-local hidden couplings (Round 15.4) (KILLED)
* **The idea**: Keep one hidden row per site (the full capacity of the rows) but make each hidden fermion's coupling column $K[:,m]$ nonzero only on a plus-shaped domain around $m$, as local-correlation methods restrict excitations to orbital domains. The Schur engine then needs no $N\times V$ table $Q=A^{-1}K$, since $A^{-1}K[\mathrm{up},S]$ is formed on demand in $\mathcal{O}(5Nb)$, cutting the accepted-move cost from $2kN(N+V)$ to $2kN^2$.
* **Lineage**: cross-field translation from quantum chemistry (local correlation), Operation B, Round 15; repaired as R15.5.
* **Source analogy**: source problem: coupled-cluster correlation energy scales as a high power of system size. Correspondence: hidden coupling column $K[:,m]$ = the domain of orbitals an excitation may reach; hidden rows = local pair correlations. Why it worked there: dynamic correlation decays with distance in gapped systems, so domain truncation (Pulay and Saebø; pair natural orbitals in DLPNO) keeps 99.9% of the correlation energy at near-linear cost. Translation gap: the sign structure (the Hard Core) is a non-local nodal property, not a decaying dynamic correlation. Citations: Pulay, Chem. Phys. Lett. 100, 151 (1983); Riplinger and Neese, J. Chem. Phys. 138, 034106 (2013).
* **Outcome**: KILLED at G0 (2026-11-17).
* **Kill rule**: Gate 0: is it known, and does it fix the reason it is not standard practice?
* **Evidence**: with $K$ local, entry $(i,j)$ of the pair-function correction is $\sum_{m\in D(i)}K[i,m]h_m\Omega_m[:,j]$, a function of the environment near $i$ only: finite-range backflow in the pairing frame. Finite-range backflow is SCALE (arXiv:2604.25775), and tensor backflow with controllable range and rank truncation is arXiv:2407.11779; SCALE's adoption barrier is the measured accuracy gap of 3 to $4\times10^{-3}t$ per site at $16\times16$ (leaf barrier P1). The operation count would pass the cost bar easily (about 14 times fewer operations than `dense2` at $L=16$), which is exactly why the literature already uses it.
* **Why this kills it**: it re-labels R1 (finite-range backflow) in another frame, and nothing in it addresses the locality accuracy gap.
* **What the kill does not rule out**: a local coupling plus an extensive non-local part that restores the missing long-range dependence (R15.5).
* **Strongest case against the kill**: the pairing reference $F_0$ and the dense readouts $\Omega_m$ are global, which SCALE's orbitals are not; but the configuration dependence, the only part that can correct the reference's nodes, is local in one index, and P1 is about exactly that; not resolved by any measurement here.
* **Checked by**: self-gated by the round-15 route generator (2026-11-17, quick literature search); no per-leaf critic yet.
* **Lesson**: L57 (`library.md`): locality and far-field analogies from dynamic correlation fail for a nodal error.

### R15.5 — Near-field plus low-rank far-field hidden couplings (Round 15.5) (KILLED)
* **The idea**: Repair R15.4 by giving the coupling a far field, as fast multipole and hierarchical matrices do: $K=K_{\mathrm{loc}}+PB$, with $K_{\mathrm{loc}}$ supported on plus-shaped domains, $P$ a cluster basis (one column per $2\times4$ cluster, so the far rank $R=V/8$ grows with $V$) and $B$ dense. The engine carries $A^{-1}$ and $Q_P=A^{-1}P[\mathrm{up},:]$ ($N\times R$) instead of $N\times V$, so long-range coupling is kept at reduced cost.
* **Lineage**: translation repair of R15.4 (replace the component: add a non-local part in place of pure locality), Round 15; also an Operation A re-gating of R13.4, whose Gate 2 kill rested on R13.1 as the foil, a foil that later failed its own cost gate. No successor.
* **Source analogy**: source problem: dense kernel matrix–vector products. Correspondence: near field = $K_{\mathrm{loc}}$; far-field low-rank blocks = $PB$. Why it worked there: far-field kernels are smooth (analytic away from the source), hence low rank at each level. Translation gap: a learned $K$ has no smoothness guarantee, and the far correlations that matter here are sign correlations, not smooth decays. Citations: Greengard and Rokhlin, J. Comput. Phys. 73, 325 (1987); Hackbusch, Computing 62, 89 (1999).
* **Outcome**: KILLED at G0 (2026-11-17).
* **Kill rule**: Gate 0 (known family with an unaddressed adoption barrier), with the Gate 4 operation count deciding which far rank is affordable.
* **Evidence**: operation count at $L=16$ with R13.1's measured move statistics (127 accepted moves and 843 ratios per sweep, $k=9.6$, $b=8$): per accepted move $2kN(N+R)+RbV$, per ratio $N^2+NRb+5Nb$. Against R13.1 ($1.35\times10^8$ multiply-adds per sweep) this is $8.6\times10^7$ at $R=V/8$ (1.57 times cheaper, predicted $R_D(16)\approx5$) but $1.27\times10^8$ at $R=V/4$ (1.06 times, no cost case), because the far part moves work from the update to every ratio ($NRb$). So the only affordable family has far rank $R/N\approx0.29$. A local-plus-low-rank backflow with controllable range and rank is published (tensor backflow, arXiv:2407.11779), and Liu and Clark (arXiv:2311.09450) show that low-rank corrections are less expressive than full backflow; that expressivity loss is the barrier.
* **Why this kills it**: the affordable member of the family is a range-and-rank truncated backflow whose rank sits where the known expressivity loss applies, and the route adds no mechanism against that loss; the repair restores long range only by re-introducing the published low-rank restriction.
* **What the kill does not rule out**: a far field whose rank is set per configuration (only where it is needed), which is the placement question R15.6 tested on rows rather than couplings; a far-field rank above $V/8$ on hardware where $NRb$ ratio products are cheap.
* **Strongest case against the kill**: the near field here is full rank and patch-nonlinear, which tensor backflow's truncation is not exactly, so the family is not identical; unresolved, and a kill-only accuracy test at $V=12$ and 15 (about 30 CPU-minutes) could still be gated if a critic overturns G0.
* **Checked by**: self-gated by the round-15 route generator (2026-11-17, quick literature search); no per-leaf critic yet.
* **Lesson**: L57 (`library.md`).

### R15.6 — Learned sparse gating of hidden rows (Round 15.6) (KILLED)
* **The idea**: Let the network decide, per configuration, which hidden rows are active: row $m$ contributes $K[:,m]\,g_m(\mathbf n)\,h_m\Omega_m$ with a patch-local gate $g_m=\mathrm{ReLU}(w\cdot x_m+c_{s(m)})$ ($c$ depends on the sublattice $s(m)$), so inactive rows are exactly zero and drop out of the carried matrix, while a penalty holds the mean active fraction at 1/2. The family contains `half` (gate set by sublattice) and hole-gated rows (gate set by occupation), so at the same active fraction it can match or beat both, while its cost follows the active count, not $V$.
* **Lineage**: Operation B (adaptive placement) after the R12.3 and R13.2 kills, which showed that fixed and hand-ruled placements both lose capacity; also a cross-field translation from conditional computation in machine learning, Round 15. No successor.
* **Source analogy**: source problem: large networks are too expensive to run in full on every input. Correspondence: hidden rows = experts; the gate $g_m$ = the router; active fraction = expert sparsity. Why it worked there: a learned router spends computation only where the input needs it, and ReLU routing with an L1 penalty gives continuous outputs with controllable sparsity. Translation gap: experts in a mixture are summed independently, while active rows here enter one determinant, so a row entering or leaving changes the carried matrix's dimension (vertex insertion and removal, as in continuous-time impurity QMC), and the cost gain needs trained gates to stay at half activity. Citations: Shazeer et al., arXiv:1701.06538 (2017); Wang, Chen and Zhu, ReLU routing for mixtures of experts, arXiv:2412.14711 (2024); Gull et al., Rev. Mod. Phys. 83, 349 (2011).
* **Outcome**: KILLED at T1 (2026-11-17).
* **Kill rule**: pre-registered (Round 15, before any run): on instance 23 ($V=12$, `full_F4` $\Delta E_{\mathrm{sign}}=7.95\times10^{-3}$), Round 12 protocol unchanged (unprojected exact-force SR, seed 11, $F=4$, plateau test) plus a constraint holding the active fraction at 0.5; KILL if $\rho(12)>1.59$ (no better than `half_F4`, its own sub-family) or if $\rho(12)>1.25$ and the run is converged; INCONCLUSIVE if the final active fraction exceeds 0.55 or the plateau is not reached within 480 CPU-seconds.
* **Evidence**: `tests/P2.1_subcubic_amplitudes_r15/`, about 7 CPU-minutes interactive. Run 1 (adaptive L1 controller) oscillated (active fraction 0.21–0.88) and was declared invalid in `RULE.md` Addendum A, written before run 2. Run 2 (a bias shift holding the active fraction at 0.5): plateau at step 750, active fraction 0.495, $E-E_0=0.142$, $\Delta E_{\mathrm{sign}}=2.05\times10^{-2}$ against `full_F4`'s $7.95\times10^{-3}$, so $\rho(12)=2.57$, against 1.59 for plain `half_F4` (whose total error is 0.078). Per accepted hop on the trained state: 3.81 rows changed and 1.75 gate flips.
* **Why this kills it**: $\rho(12)=2.57>1.59$ fires the first clause: at half activity the learned placement ended worse than the fixed sublattice it contains, in sign error and in total error, so the route has no accuracy case at $V=12$ and its cost case (which needed it to beat `half`) is moot.
* **What the kill does not rule out**: a gate initialised at the sublattice pattern (which guarantees at least `half`'s value, then can only improve), other active fractions, wider rows, and other gate optimisers; the measured update statistics (3.81 rows and 1.75 flips per hop) would still allow a cost case if accuracy were recovered.
* **Strongest case against the kill**: the family contains `half`, so ending worse than `half` is an optimiser failure (L33), not a capacity limit. Unresolved; but `half` itself is killed (R11.1, R12.3), so matching it would not pass the route, which needs $\rho\le1.25$, and nothing in the run approached that.
* **Checked by**: orchestrator, 2026-11-17 (pre-registered rule applied; Addendum A registered before run 2); no per-leaf critic yet.
* **Lesson**: L33 (`library.md`): separately trained arms compare optimisers as well as families; here a superset trained from scratch ended below its own subfamily.

### R15.7 — Multiple-try Metropolis on cheap candidate ratios (Round 15.7) (KILLED)
* **The idea**: The all-sites route's ratios are about 40 times cheaper than `dense2`'s, which pays an LU per candidate. Multiple-try Metropolis evaluates $M$ candidate hops per step and picks one by weight, so the route could spend its cheap ratios on better-chosen moves and lower the autocorrelation time per sweep, an advantage `dense2` cannot afford.
* **Lineage**: Operation B (active probing) and a cross-field translation from Monte Carlo statistics, Round 15. No successor.
* **Source analogy**: source problem: random-walk Metropolis mixes slowly. Correspondence: candidate hops = trial points; carried-inverse ratios = target-density evaluations. Why it worked there: several cheap trial evaluations per step increase the accepted step's quality when evaluations are cheap relative to the step. Translation gap: here the expensive part is the accepted update, not the evaluation. Citation: Liu, Liang and Wong, J. Am. Stat. Assoc. 95, 121 (2000).
* **Outcome**: KILLED at G4 (2026-11-17, T0).
* **Kill rule**: Gate 4: can the mechanism lower the route's cost per decorrelated sample at $L=16$ by the needed factor?
* **Evidence**: on the R13.1 engine about 74% of the sweep is accepted-move updates ($127\times7.9\times10^5$ of $1.35\times10^8$ multiply-adds), and on the 11c engine about 70% (R13.6). With acceptance 0.15 the route spends about 6.6 ratios per accepted move; multiple-try Metropolis with $M$ candidates needs $2M$ ratios per step (forward and reference sets), more per accepted move. A single-hop accepted move moves one electron by one bond whichever candidate is chosen, so decorrelation per accepted move, which sets the dominant cost, does not grow with $M$; selecting by $\lvert\psi\rvert^2$ weight favours high-weight, often reversing, moves.
* **Why this kills it**: the gain is bounded by the ratio share of the sweep (at most about 1.35 times, the R13.6 bound), and multiple tries increase that share instead of shrinking it.
* **What the kill does not rule out**: multiple tries over composite moves that decorrelate more per accepted update than their update rank costs; delayed acceptance for families where ratios dominate (R13.6's open scope).
* **Strongest case against the kill**: trained states may have much larger integrated autocorrelation times than the untrained ones measured (about 1 to 1.8 sweeps), where better proposal choice could matter; unresolved, but any gain must still come per accepted update, which the argument bounds.
* **Checked by**: self-gated by the round-15 route generator (2026-11-17, quick literature search found no multiple-try Metropolis in NQS VMC); no per-leaf critic yet.
* **Lesson**: route-specific: the accepted update, not the ratio, dominates this family's sweep (the same mechanism as R2.5 and R13.6), which is a property of this engine, not a general lesson.

## Leaf Status Summary
* **State**: OPEN
* **Updated**: 2026-11-17 (round 15: seven routes; R15.2, R15.3, R15.7 KILLED at G4, R15.4 and R15.5 KILLED at G0, R15.6 KILLED at T1 ($\rho(12)=2.57$); R15.1 LIVE, gated job 742029 running). Earlier the same day: job 731409, R12.3 KILLED at T1 by the trend clause ($\rho=0.038$, 0.886, 1.110, 1.915 at $V=8$, 12, 14, 15); R13.7 stays SHELVED.
* **Question**: Can the amplitude ratios $\psi(\mathbf{n}')/\psi(\mathbf{n})$ needed for local energies of a backflow-type state be computed in $o(N_e^3)$ per configuration (amortized), for example by exploiting locality of backflow, without changing the variational family?
* **Answer**: Partly. For networks whose extra internal rows are built from local patches of the lattice, we derived and checked an exact method that computes the needed ratios in about $N_e^2$ instead of $N_e^3$ operations. At the sizes we can reach it only saves real time if the network keeps fewer extra rows than there are lattice sites, and that does change the family: every such reduced version got the wave function's signs worse on 12- to 15-site clusters. One last test, whether that loss is just incomplete training, is running.
* **Conclusion**: An exact local energy costing $\mathcal{O}(N_e^2)$ instead of $\mathcal{O}(N_e^3)$ or more is achievable and verified for determinant and pairing NQS with patch-local hidden rows; but at reachable sizes it pays only if the carried matrix is made smaller than one hidden row per site, and every way of doing that tested so far (fixed sublattices, hole-gated rows) lost sign quality on 12- to 15-site clusters. Checkerboard rows with four times more features per row also lost: their sign-error ratio to the all-sites route rose to 1.92 at 15 sites (job 731409), and a learned per-configuration choice of active rows did worse than the fixed checkerboard (2.57 against 1.59 at 12 sites). The one live route asks whether the 15-site loss is an optimiser artefact (R15.1, a deterministic polish of both arms, job 742029 running).

**The question.** Neural-network backflow (NNBF), the accurate neural wave function for lattice fermions, makes each amplitude a determinant of a matrix that a network computes from the whole configuration. A local energy needs the amplitudes of all $\mathcal{O}(zN_e)$ configurations one hop away ($z$ neighbours, $N_e$ electrons), so it costs $\mathcal{O}(N_e^4)$, against $\mathcal{O}(N_e^2)$ for the Minimal Contrast Foil (projected BCS or Slater times a Jastrow factor). The leaf asks for an antisymmetric family with an exact local energy below $\mathcal{O}(N_e^3)$, a Monte Carlo sweep at the Foil's $\mathcal{O}(N_e^3)$, and no loss of accuracy at the $10^{-3}t$ per-site scale of competing orders. A one-line bound (F1) shows this is impossible for unrestricted backflow: the family must change by low rank under a hop.

**The mechanism.** Give each site a "hidden fermion" (an extra row of an augmented matrix) whose entries depend only on a small patch of the configuration, and couple these rows to the physical orbitals through a learned linear map. A hop then changes only the $b\approx10$ rows whose patch it touches, so each ratio is a $b$-dimensional determinant from a carried inverse, and decoupled subsystems factorise (size consistency). This family (R6.1′ in the Slater frame, R7.1 in the pairing frame, R7.3 with a sparse hidden–hidden block) was verified exact to $10^{-14}$ with drift below $6\times10^{-11}$ over $10^4$ accepted moves.

**The chain of evidence.** (1) Earlier sum-of-terms families (R3.1, R3.2) worked at 16 sites, 236 to 594 times cheaper per connection, but a sum of fixed-degree terms cannot represent a sign that is a parity over decoupled blocks, so they were killed at Gate 4. (2) The local-hidden-row routes beat NNBF at 75 SR steps and matched parameters, but NNBF improves 5.6 times between steps 75 and 1200, so those ratios measured learning speed (R6.1″, R7.3), and a wider NNBF that is faster per step won outright on instance 4. (3) Cost per decorrelated sample (sweep time times integrated autocorrelation time) against the dense pairing state with the same features was $0.99\pm0.56$ at $L=16$ (8c): no cost case. The Round 11 critic closed the leaf on an update-bottleneck lemma. (4) Round 11c falsified that lemma: the bottleneck was carried tables the sweep does not need. With a compiled inverse-only engine, the checkerboard variant `half` ($V_h=V/2$) is $5.05\pm1.27$ times and `quarter` $10.3\pm2.5$ times cheaper than dense HFPS at $L=16$, while the all-sites route reaches only $1.78\pm0.52$ (and $3.50\pm0.85$, then $2.02\pm0.80$ at $L=24$, with the Schur engine of R13.1). (5) Accuracy at fixed width $F=4$, unprojected SR to a plateau on OBC $t$–$t'$–$J$ toys with $V=8$ to 15: `half` is 1.6 to 2.4 times worse in $\Delta E_{\mathrm{sign}}$ than the all-sites route at every size, `quarter` 1.9 to 9.9 times (job 704752, KILL), and hole-gated rows (R13.2) are 0.83, 1.37 and 2.04 times at $V=8$, 12 and 15 (partial job 707185; the kill clause fires on complete inputs, upheld by the per-leaf critic). Its wider mutation R13.7 ($F=8$) was not run: its pre-registered run condition (every triggering $\rho\le1.5$) failed, which shelves it rather than refutes it. (6) `half` at $F=16$ keeps $R_D=5.34\pm1.47$ (12c) but gives $\rho=0.04$, 0.89, 1.11 and 1.92 at $V=8$, 12, 14 and 15 (jobs 706240 and 731409), a strict rise ending above both the 1.15 kill line and the 1.5 marginal line: KILLED at T1 by the pre-registered trend clause (R12.3), although it had 1.6 to 1.8 times `full_F4`'s parameters.

**Why the alternatives fail.** Fixed-rank or summed global channels are not size-consistent (R6.1, R7.4, R3.1); products of normalised ratios have extensive poles (R6.2); hierarchies and deeper trunks cannot be tested at exact-sum size (R2.6, R3.4, R6.4, R7.2); cheaper engines for the all-sites wave function miss the cost bar (R13.1, R13.1′).

**Strongest objection and answer.** All cost numbers use untrained parameters with $\tau_{\mathrm{int}}\approx1$ to 1.8 sweeps, and all accuracy numbers are single-seed and unprojected on clusters of at most 15 sites. Trained states may decorrelate more slowly, which would move every cost crossover outward, never inward, and a single seed cannot exclude that `half_F16`'s 1.11 at $V=14$ is noise. The answer is scope, not refutation: no claim here extends to trained VMC at $L\ge16$.

**Limits.** No accuracy test at the deployment scale ($L\ge16$, variational Monte Carlo without an exact reference) was run; ED-size accuracy is at most 15 sites; projected comparisons were done only for the earlier routes.

* **Scope**: cost on $L\times L$ periodic $t$–$t'$–$J$ at doping $1/8$, $L=8$ to 24, single-threaded CPU, untrained parameters; accuracy on OBC $t$–$t'$–$J$ toys with 8 to 15 sites ($t'=-0.25t$, $J=0.4t$) and, for the earlier routes, projected SR on $4\times4$ and $5\times3$ clusters.
* **Compute spent**: 9,524 CPU-hours on 10 cluster jobs in the historical ledger (before the compute gate), 8,906 of them in the round-5 decisive array 526080 whose verdict changed no decision; the cancelled 706240 (144 CPU-hours committed in the ledger, about 0.75 CPU-hour actually used) and 707185 (about 1 CPU-hour); about 4 CPU-hours of local T0 and T1 runs, plus about 7 CPU-minutes for the R15.6 T1; 0.62 of the 300 CPU-hours of the new per-leaf budget (job 731409), with job 742029 (cap 0.42 CPU-h) running.
* **What this leaf taught beyond itself** (entries of `library.md`):
    * L53 (R3.1, R3.2, R6.1, R6.2, R7.4): sums of a fixed number of terms and fixed-size hidden-fermion factors cannot carry a block-parity sign, and products of ratios sharing one reference are singular; independent block corrections need a product inside one determinant. L10 (R6.3): a fixed projection bonus is a finite-size gain.
    * L39 (R2.6, R3.4, R6.4, R7.2): mechanisms whose value is a local-versus-global difference cannot be tested on exact-sum clusters.
    * L33 (R6.1′, R6.1″, R7.3; absorbed L34 on 2026-11-17): short-horizon matched-parameter accuracy ratios measure learning speed, so they never promote a cost route, and a kill from them stands only with a converged or equal-wall-time arm.
    * L28 (R11.1, R11.2, R13.2; R13.7 is now shelved, not a kill): fewer than one hidden row per site loses sign capacity on 12- to 15-site clusters.
    * L47 (R13.1, R13.1′, R13.3, Round 11 diagnostic): predict cost gates from measured kernel speeds and from the minimal set of carried tables, not from multiply-add counts of one implementation.
    * L12 (R4): single-proposal stochastic ratio estimators have a variance floor that diverges near nodes.
    * L57 (R15.4, R15.5): analogies from dynamic correlation (domain-local correlation, fast-multipole far fields) fail for an error that sits in non-local nodal structure, and making the far part affordable forces it back into the published low-rank restriction.
    * L58 (R15.2, with the measured update ranks of R11.1, R13.1 and R15.6): in patch-local hidden-row families the update rank is the number of rows whose receptive field contains a touched site, so thinning rows while widening receptive fields saves only the matrix dimension.
* **Most promising direction**: R15.1, the polished re-score of `half_F16` against `full_F4` at $V=15$ (LIVE, job 742029). Mechanism: both arms were scored at the best point of a noisy SR trajectory, and the larger `half_F16` sat further above its late-trajectory floor (0.009 against 0.003), so an exact-gradient polish of both arms could shrink $\rho(15)=1.915$ if the loss is an optimiser artefact (L33). For: a 5-iteration probe already moved $\rho$ to about 1.78. Against: at $V=15$ width bought total energy but no sign capacity (`half` $2.66\to2.83\times10^{-3}$ from $F=4$ to 16), and L2 found trained sign errors capacity-limited; KILL is predicted with $p\approx0.7$. R13.7 (hole-gated rows at $F=8$) stays SHELVED. The mechanism every surviving idea must supply: keep the carried matrix below one hidden row per site without losing sign capacity, which neither more features per row ($F=16$), fixed or hole-gated row subsets, nor learned gating (R15.6) achieved at 12 to 15 sites.
* **Next step**: job 742029 (R15.1, T1, cap 0.42 CPU-h, `tests/P2.1_subcubic_amplitudes_r15b/`), running. Pre-registered: KILL if $\rho_{\mathrm{pol}}(15)>1.25$; if $\le1.15$, polish $V=12$ and 14 the same way and apply the Round 12d trend rule to the four polished ratios; between, NOT RESOLVED (per-leaf critic). KILL → no LIVE route, and with Round 13 and Round 15 both refilled and killed (including a cross-field translation and its repair) the leaf goes DORMANT with the Saturation Diagnostic below; PASS → the polished ladder at $V=12$ and 14.
* **What would close it**: if R15.1 dies, the idea-saturation trigger of section 3b fires (Rounds 13 and 15 both killed), and the leaf goes DORMANT with a Saturation Diagnostic ("the sub-cubic sweep pays at reachable sizes only by removing hidden rows, and removing rows, even with four times more features per row, costs sign capacity at 12 to 15 sites"), whose falsifier is an equal-wall-time VMC accuracy test at $L\ge16$, with R13.7 as a shelved ledger row. A route that passes T1 and then an equal-wall-time T2 on instance 4 would keep it OPEN; ANSWERED would need that comparison to hold at $L\ge16$.
